The animation-derived stack is anticipation -> contact -> reaction/follow-through, with a fourth stage that Vlambeer calls PERMANENCE (step 13, and again at step 30 'increased permanence'). Mapped to combat: ANTICIPATION is the windup, the muzzle flash and the pre-hit telegraph; CONTACT is the frame of impact — hitstop, flash, spark, sound; REACTION is the 100-400ms afterwards — knockback, sprite shake, damage number, recoil; AFTERMATH is what persists for seconds or forever — corpses, blood, scorch marks, destroyed terrain, shell casings. Most games over-invest in CONTACT and under-invest in ANTICIPATION and AFTERMATH, which are the two stages that make a world feel physical rather than reactive. The mush threshold: when more than one GLOBAL channel fires per hit, the channels stop being separable. Two simultaneous global effects (shake + flash, or shake + hitstop) at high hit rates produce the 'everything is exploding and I can't see' failure.

**Apply to Shardfall.** Adopt an explicit per-hit budget: at most 1 GLOBAL channel (hitstop OR camera shake OR full-screen flash — and hitstop should win nearly always), at most 3 TARGET-LOCAL channels (sprite offset, colour flash, knockback), at most 2 SPAWNED channels (impact VFX, damage number), and exactly 1 audio cue. Seven total, one global. Then audit your stages: with everything rendering as flat fillRect quads, your ANTICIPATION stage is probably empty — a 6-10 frame (100-167ms) windup tell on every grunt attack, drawn as a 1px outline colour shift or a small scale-up of the enemy quad, costs nothing and is worth more than any amount of shake tuning. Your AFTERMATH stage has huge free upside because your terrain is already destructible: scorch marks on tiles, corpses that persist for the room, projectiles embedded in walls. That is Vlambeer's step 13, and it precedes shake in his ordering for a reason.

*Source: Channel enumeration is a direct read of Hades CombatPresentation.lua DamagePresentation. The anticipation/contact/reaction/aftermath framing is standard animation principle (Disney 12 principles, restated in Swink, Game Feel, 2009). The 'one global channel' rule is MY PROPOSAL, inferred from the Hades data.*

#### Accessibility that improves feel — the three-state pattern and the free web win

**Numbers.** Celeste ships Screen Shake as a graded setting DEFAULTING TO 50%, not a binary toggle, plus a separate Photosensitive Mode that disables screen flashes. Hades gates damage numbers behind ConfigOptionCache.ShowDamageNumbers, and Celeste Classic's shake is gated on Settings.Instance.DisableScreenShake. Flash safety thresholds: WCAG 2.3.1 — no more than three flashes in any one-second period, or below the general/red flash thresholds. Game Accessibility Guidelines — flashing sequences over 5 seconds, or more than three flashes per second covering 25%+ of the screen, are seizure triggers. Xbox Accessibility Guideline 118 — no more than ~3 flashes/second and not covering ~20%+ of the screen. Red flashing is more dangerous than other hues. Naming guidance: do NOT label a setting 'epilepsy safe mode' (legal exposure); label it 'Screen Flash Effects' or 'Effects Intensity'.

The Celeste default of 50% is the interesting data point: the shipping default is half of the designer's maximum, which implies the maximum was tuned as a ceiling for players who want it rather than as the intended experience. A graded slider is also better engineering than a toggle — it is a single multiplier on trauma, so it costs one line, whereas a toggle tempts you to write two code paths. Your per-frame random shake at 60fps is 30Hz motion, and your existing 2-16 amplitude range at a 272px view width means large events displace a substantial fraction of the screen — both are worth checking against the 3-flashes/25%-of-screen rule, particularly if you have any full-screen white/red flash on crit or on player damage.

**Apply to Shardfall.** Because you are a single HTML file, you get reduced-motion detection FREE: `matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)')`. Use it to set the initial shake multiplier to 0 and the flash-alpha multiplier to 0.35, then let the player override. Ship three sliders, all of which are single multipliers on values you already have: Screen Shake 0-100% (default 50%, Celeste's default), Screen Flash 0-100% (default 100%, 0% when reduced-motion is set), Hit Stop 0-150% (default 100%). Add Show Damage Numbers as a toggle. Hard rules to enforce in code: no full-screen flash may exceed 3 per second; no full-screen flash may use saturated red at high alpha; any flash covering more than 25% of the canvas must be capped at 2 per second. Name the settings by what they do, never 'epilepsy mode'.

*Source: Celeste settings and changelog (celestegame.com, maddymakesgamesinc.itch.io v1.4.0.0 changelog); gameaccessibilityguidelines.com 'Avoid flickering images and repetitive patterns'; W3C WCAG 2.2 Understanding SC 2.3.1; Microsoft Learn Xbox Accessibility Guideline 118. High confidence.*

#### Accessibility — colourblind-safe damage colours without a second art pass

**Numbers.** Okabe-Ito 8-colour palette (Color Universal Design, 2002; popularised by Bang Wong, Nature Methods 2011): #E69F00 orange, #56B4E9 sky blue, #009E73 bluish green, #F0E442 yellow, #0072B2 blue, #D55E00 vermilion, #CC79A7 reddish purple, #000000 black. Distinguishable under protanopia, deuteranopia and tritanopia, and separable in greyscale because the colours differ in luminance as well as hue.

The 'no second art pass' requirement is satisfiable because Okabe-Ito varies LUMINANCE, not just hue — so a palette swap is genuinely a palette swap, not a redesign. The complementary technique, and the one Hades demonstrates, is REDUNDANT ENCODING: crits are signalled by size (1.78x), a glyph ('!'), and a sound before colour is involved at all. Any information you encode in hue alone is information you have hidden from ~8% of male players. The classic failure in an ARPG is red-vs-green damage types, and red-vs-green item rarity.

**Apply to Shardfall.** You have 62 gems across skill/support/aura/ability and multiple damage types — a lot of colour-coded taxonomy. Map your damage types onto Okabe-Ito directly: physical #FFFFFF (white, keep it neutral), fire #D55E00 vermilion, cold #56B4E9 sky blue, lightning #F0E442 yellow, chaos #CC79A7 reddish purple, poison #009E73 bluish green. Note vermilion and bluish green are the SAFE substitutes for the naive red and green. For item rarity use the same trick plus a redundant non-colour cue (border thickness in pixels: 1/2/3/4 for normal/magic/rare/unique). For crits, follow Hades exactly: size first, glyph second, sound third, colour last. Enforce a minimum luminance contrast on every damage number against your darkest and lightest biome backgrounds — with 6 biomes you will otherwise have a biome where yellow lightning numbers vanish.

*Source: Okabe & Ito, Color Universal Design (2002); Wong, Nature Methods 8:441 (2011). High confidence — these hex values are standard and widely reproduced.*

#### Hit-feedback preferences — a recent user-calibration study

**Numbers.** CHI 2026 Extended Abstracts: participants directly adjusted 8 visual and temporal hit-feedback parameters in a 2D action game. Findings reported: preferences are consistent across attack DISTANCES but differ significantly by attack TYPE, with charge attacks wanting 1.7x stronger presentation; individual differences span a 6-fold range with three distinct preference clusters; RANDOM parameter settings were judged excessive 45% of the time, meaning comfortable ranges are biased toward the LOWER end of the plausible parameter space; settings averaged from 16 participants achieved >80% 'optimal' ratings from new users.

Three usable takeaways. (1) The 6-fold individual spread is the strongest available argument for shipping intensity sliders rather than a single tuned value — no single number satisfies the population. (2) 'Random settings perceived as excessive 45% of the time' is a direct empirical warning that developers systematically over-tune juice; if you are unsure between two values, the lower one is more likely correct. (3) The 1.7x factor for charge attacks is a concrete ratio for heavy-vs-light presentation strength, and it lines up neatly with Hades' 1.78x crit font ratio and with Guilty Gear Xrd's 10f-vs-7f hitstop ratio of 1.43x. A heavy hit should be roughly 1.4-1.8x a light hit across every channel, not 3x.

**Apply to Shardfall.** Use 1.7x as your heavy-to-light ratio across the board and check your existing numbers against it: your hitstop light 0.05 to heavy 0.08 is 1.6x — good, that one is right. Your shake range 2 to 16 is 8x — far outside the ratio and further evidence the top end is too high. Damage number crit ratio should be ~1.7x (Hades is 1.78x). And given the 6-fold individual spread and the 45% over-tuning result: pick your values at the conservative end and ship the three intensity sliders described in the accessibility finding, which turns a tuning problem you cannot win into a settings menu.

*Source: dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3772363.3798911, 'Quantifying Hit Feedback Preferences in 2D Action Games: A User-Adjustment Study of Visual and Temporal Parameters', CHI 2026 EA. Medium confidence — I could only reach the abstract and search snippets, not the full parameter tables, so the 8 parameters and their ranges are not verified.*

### Recommendations

- VERDICT TABLE on your eight numbers. KEEP AS-IS: coyote 0.1s (exactly Celeste's JumpGraceTime). KEEP THE CONSTANT, FIX THE MATH: camera lerp 5 (Celeste's effective rate is 4.605/s — but change to cam += (target-cam)*(1-Math.exp(-5*dt)) with dt clamped to 1/30). KEEP BUT EXTEND: jump buffer 0.12s is in the 80-160ms good band, but must also cover attack/dash/cast, and must not tick down during hitstop. RESTRUCTURE: hitstop 0.05/0.08/0.18 — the magnitudes are defensible but the model is wrong (see next item). CUT HARD: screenshake 2-16 — 16px on a 272px view is 5.9% of screen width vs Hades' 0.4% and Celeste Classic's 1.6%; cap at 8px. REPLACE: decay 30/s linear — use trauma^2 with trauma -= 1.5*dt. WIDEN: 17 tiles is roughly 2x more zoomed-in than Celeste while having flight and ranged enemies; go to 22-24 tiles or add dynamic pull-out. RAISE: 6 voices to 16 with per-class caps and a 35ms retrigger lockout.
- Rewrite hitstop as a time-scale system with four fields per event (preDelay, fraction, hold, lerpBack), copying Hades' shipped structure. Proposed table: light {0.016, 0.25, 0.020, 0.030}; medium {0.016, 0.15, 0.040, 0.050}; heavy/crit {0.024, 0.08, 0.070, 0.070}; grunt kill {0.016, 0.10, 0.060, 0.060}; miniboss kill {0.024, 0.02, 0.130, 0.120}; boss kill {0.030, 0.005, 0.220, 0.250} plus a 0.35x slow-mo tail for 0.5s; PLAYER TAKES DAMAGE {0.020, 0.02, 0.120, 0.060}. The two non-negotiable details: the pre-delay (impact frame plays at full speed first) and the partial fraction (never a hard 0). Enforce max() not sum(), one active slow at a time, and a 0.08s per-source cooldown.
- Add the player-damage hitstop you are missing. Hades gives dealing damage ~10-40ms at 10% speed and TAKING damage ~140ms at 1% speed — a 4-14x asymmetry. This single addition will do more for how your combat reads than any screenshake tuning, and it is the mechanism that makes death feel earned rather than arbitrary in a roguelite.
- Stop shaking the camera on ordinary hits. Port Hades' two local channels instead: offset the enemy's own quad by up to 3px for 0.12-0.15s (Hades: Distance 6, Speed 550, Duration 0.15) and flash the quad bright for 30ms (Hades: Flash Duration 0.03, one cycle). In Hades only 3 of 207 enemy entries shake the screen on death, all explosive. With 13 grunt types and swarms, a local flash tells the player WHICH enemy was hit; camera shake cannot.
- Build a requestShake(trauma, sourcePos, axis) funnel that applies distance falloff (trauma *= 1/(1+(dist/(viewW*0.75))^2)), a 150ms max()-window against repeats, a reduced-motion check, and a menu check, before touching the trauma value. Give shake a semantic axis (vertical for quakes, along the damage vector for hits) as Hades does with its Angle parameter. Make absolutely certain aura and DoT damage cannot reach this function.
- Implement damage numbers to Hades' shipped spec, re-based for your view size: draw in SCREEN space at 13 CSS px base and 22 CSS px for crits (1.69x, matching Hades' 1.78x); magnitude scale bands {<25: 0.85}, {25-74: 1.0}, {75-199: 1.15}, {200-599: 1.3}, {600-1999: 1.45}, {2000+: 1.6}; 3 per row, 11px row spacing, 18px column spacing; spawn jitter ±6 X / ±2 Y; lifetime 0.25s hold + 1.0s drift + 0.2s + 0.25s fade. The highest-value single behaviour: SHRINK to 0.75x and darken at +0.1s so older numbers recede. COALESCE by (victimId, damageSourceKey) with a 0.25s hold window — mandatory given auras, supports and DoTs. Global cap 24 live numbers. Never show a floating number for damage taken — use a directional edge vignette instead.
- Fix the camera integrator and add lookahead. Change to exponential form with clamped dt; clamp the TARGET to world bounds before smoothing (not the result); freeze the camera entirely while timeScale < 1; apply shake AFTER the bounds clamp. Add targetX += clamp(vx * 0.28, -56, +56) (±20% of a 272px view; Celeste's max is 15%, the classic Mario/Defender figure is 25%) and asymmetric targetY += clamp(vy * 0.16, -32, +56) so you see further down when descending. Add a 24x20px deadzone so recoil does not drag the camera. Use k=12 vertically when grounded with a snap on landing.
- Widen the view. 17 tiles = 272px means your 12x20 player occupies 13% of screen height — roughly twice Celeste's ratio, in a game with flight, ranged enemies and 3200 tiles of descent. Either set the base view to 22-24 tiles, or lerp view width from 17 to 22 tiles over 0.6s driven by max(|vx|,|vy|)/vmax so melee stays tight and flight opens up.
- Restructure audio as 8 synthesis primitives producing ~34 perceptually distinct event classes, with everything else expressed as continuous parameters rather than new cues (Hades ships 501 discrete cues, which is the wrong model for procedural audio). Ship a single hit(materialIndex, weight, isCrit, pan) function where weight in [0,1] lerps body start frequency 300->620Hz, body decay 60->140ms, transient bandpass 4200->2600Hz. Pan by screen position via StereoPannerNode clamped to ±0.8 — spatialised hits disambiguate swarms for free.
- Use this 3-layer recipe for the hit sound, then tune by ear: transient = 12ms white noise through bandpass 3500Hz Q=1.2, gain 0->1 in 0.5ms then exponential to 0.001 over 12ms; body = sine/triangle/square by material, frequency exponentialRamp 420Hz->90Hz over 45ms, gain 0->0.8 in 2ms then exponential decay over 90ms; tail = noise through lowpass sweeping 6000->700Hz over 120ms at gain 0.25 decaying over 140ms. Metal adds two resonant sines at 1900/2850Hz Q=18 decaying 250ms. The pitch envelope on layer 2 is where the punch comes from; the transient is what keeps it audible on phone speakers. Always exponentialRampToValueAtTime (target 0.001, never 0), never linear, and always schedule against audioCtx.currentTime on the same frame as the visual.
- Raise the voice budget to 16 with per-class caps (hits 4, projectiles 3, player 2, world 3, UI 2, ambient 2), a 35ms per-class retrigger lockout, and +1.5dB gain compensation (capped +4dB) applied to the playing instance when a voice is suppressed. Add a DynamicsCompressorNode on the SFX bus (threshold -10, knee 6, ratio 12, attack 0.003, release 0.10). Pitch-randomise ±1.5 semitones for enemy hits, ±0.8 for player actions, ±2.5 for world; use a rising ladder (min(combo,8) * 0.4 semitones) rather than random for combo chains, which gives you free combo feedback with no UI.
- Adopt an explicit per-hit feedback budget: at most 1 GLOBAL channel (hitstop wins over shake and flash), at most 3 target-local (sprite offset, colour flash, knockback), at most 2 spawned (impact VFX, damage number), exactly 1 audio cue. Then audit the four stages — anticipation, contact, reaction, aftermath. Your anticipation stage is almost certainly empty: add a 6-10 frame (100-167ms) windup tell to every grunt attack, drawn as an outline or a small quad scale-up. Your aftermath stage has the most free upside because your terrain is already destructible: scorch marks, persistent corpses, projectiles embedded in walls. Vlambeer's ordering puts permanence at step 13 and screenshake at step 16.
- Use 1.7x as the universal heavy-to-light presentation ratio, corroborated three independent ways: the CHI 2026 study's 1.7x for charge attacks, Hades' 1.78x crit font ratio, and Guilty Gear Xrd's 1.43x hitstop ratio. Your hitstop light-to-heavy ratio of 1.6x already fits; your shake range of 8x does not.
- Add the missing input-forgiveness constants from Celeste: corner correction at 5px (Celeste uses 4px on an 8px-wide player = 50%; your player is 12px wide so 5px is the equivalent ratio, and this is critical in destructible terrain), a ceiling variable-jump grace of 0.05s (Celeste's CeilingVarJumpGrace), an apex half-gravity threshold (Celeste's HalfGravThreshold = 40 with Gravity 900), air control at ~0.65x ground control (Celeste's AirMult), and — because you have flight — a 0.06-0.10s grace after flight fuel depletes before full gravity resumes.
- Ship three intensity sliders plus one toggle, each a single multiplier on values you already compute: Screen Shake 0-100% defaulting to 50% (Celeste's shipping default), Screen Flash 0-100% (default 0% when prefers-reduced-motion is set), Hit Stop 0-150%, and Show Damage Numbers on/off (Hades gates its own damage numbers this way). Detect reduced motion via matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)') — free in a single-HTML-file game. Name settings by what they do, never 'epilepsy mode'.
- Enforce flash safety in code, not by convention: no full-screen flash more than 3 times per second, none using saturated red at high alpha, and any flash covering >25% of the canvas capped at 2 per second (WCAG 2.3.1, Game Accessibility Guidelines, Xbox AG 118). Related: replace per-frame Math.random() shake offsets (30Hz white noise at 60fps) with smooth noise sampled at 18-25Hz — three summed sines at 14/23/37 Hz is enough — and round the final camera offset to whole device pixels so 16px tiles do not shimmer.
- Map damage types onto Okabe-Ito so the palette is colourblind-safe with no second art pass (it varies luminance, not just hue): physical #FFFFFF, fire #D55E00 vermilion, cold #56B4E9 sky blue, lightning #F0E442 yellow, chaos #CC79A7 reddish purple, poison #009E73 bluish green. Note vermilion and bluish green are the safe substitutes for naive red and green. Encode item rarity redundantly with border thickness 1/2/3/4 px alongside colour. Check every damage-number colour for luminance contrast against all 6 biome backgrounds.
- Working files if useful: Hades' shipped Lua at /tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Sweet-Spot/00ff769b-1474-43f1-895c-6b76d1865b29/scratchpad/hades/Scripts (CombatPresentation.lua for DisplayDamageText/DoUnitShake/DoUnitHitFlash/AddSimSpeedChange, WeaponData.lua for the 242 HitSimSlowParameters blocks, HeroData.lua line 8926 for DamageNumberTextScales, UIData.lua line 2491 for CombatUI); Celeste at /tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Sweet-Spot/00ff769b-1474-43f1-895c-6b76d1865b29/scratchpad/celeste (Source/Player/Player.cs for the physics constants and CameraTarget, Source/PICO-8/Classic.cs for the Classic freeze/shake/grace/jbuffer values); the extracted impact-feel paper text at /tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Sweet-Spot/00ff769b-1474-43f1-895c-6b76d1865b29/scratchpad/impact.txt.

### Caveats and unsourced claims

- The Vlambeer 'Art of Screenshake' talk publishes NO numeric values — not amplitude, not duration, not frame counts. I checked the talk, two community recreations and the transcript-annotation pages. Anyone citing specific numbers to that talk is fabricating them. What it legitimately provides is the ordered 32-step progression with timestamps, which I have included. Do not put numbers in a design table attributed to Vlambeer.
- There is no public Supergiant GDC talk on Hades combat feel with numbers. Every Hades value in this report is read directly out of the game's shipped Lua scripts, which is stronger evidence than a talk would be — but it means the values are undocumented and uncommented, and my interpretation of semantics (e.g. that ScreenPreWait on the second entry is the hold duration, that Distance is in 1080p screen pixels) is inference from the code structure, not from developer statement. Confidence high but not certain.
- Motion Twin's GDC 2019 Dead Cells deck contains no numbers at all — I downloaded and extracted all 23 pages. It is entirely qualitative ('just-in-time jumps', 'trying to understand player's intention', 'free turn-around', 'auto-aiming'). There is a separate 2018 TGDF talk by the same developer that I did not extract; if you want Dead Cells values specifically, that video is the remaining lead. Do not cite Dead Cells for numeric hitstop or coyote values on my authority.
- The Street Fighter V hitstop values (8/12/15 frames for light/medium/heavy) are documented ON BLOCK. I could not reach the primary Shoryuken article (TLS failure) to confirm whether on-hit values differ, and in most fighting games they do. Treat the on-hit/on-block distinction as unverified.
- The Celeste camera smoothing line (1 - Math.pow(0.01f, deltaTime)) comes from the DECOMPILED 1.4.0.0 build and community reproductions, not from the officially released source — Noel Berry only released Player.cs and the PICO-8 port, not Level.cs. The Player.cs constants, the CameraTarget method and all Celeste Classic values ARE from official source and are solid.
- The CHI 2026 hit-feedback study is the most directly relevant work I found, but I could only reach its abstract and search snippets — ACM returned 403. The 1.7x charge-attack factor, the 6-fold individual spread and the 45%-perceived-excessive figure are from the abstract; the actual eight parameters, their ranges and the mean preferred values are NOT verified. If this study matters to your tables, get the full text.
- All the synthesis frequencies, times and gain values in the punchy-hit recipe are MY PROPOSAL. They are grounded in standard percussion-synthesis practice (pitch envelope, noise transient, exponential decay) but no game published them. Tune by ear and expect to move them 20-40%.
- My screenshake trauma constants (maxShakePx 8, decay 1.5/s, the specific trauma bumps), the camera lookahead coefficients (0.28 horizontal, 0.16 vertical), the distance-falloff formula, the per-action buffer table, the voice-budget caps, the 34-event-class audio target and the per-hit channel budget are all MY PROPOSALS, calibrated against the sourced values but not measured from any shipped game. They are starting points for playtesting, not citations.
- One implementation hazard worth flagging separately because it will silently break things: if your hitstop pauses the whole update loop, then your 0.12s jump buffer, your 0.1s coyote timer and your damage-number lifetimes are all counting down in scaled time. A 0.16s heavy-hit hitstop will eat an entire input buffer. Buffer and forgiveness timers must run on unscaled time, and damage numbers must render on unscaled time (Hades spawns them on an object literally named BlankObstacleNoTimeModifier for exactly this reason).
- A view-width change from 17 to 22-24 tiles is not a pure feel change — it alters enemy engagement ranges, projectile lead times, how much destructible terrain is on screen (and therefore your fill-rate cost on mobile), and your 8-12 minute target run length. Prototype it against the threat tiers before committing, and be aware it interacts with your depthMul enemy scaling: a wider view makes ranged enemies at high depth substantially less dangerous.
